<div id="A1.SSx3.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="A1.SSx3.p1.1" class="ltx_p">Table <a href="#A1.T3" title="Table 3 ‣ Dataset Statistics ‣ Appendix A Appendix ‣ IdeaBench: Benchmarking Large Language Models for Research Idea Generation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a> shows the total number of target papers in our dataset along with the number of filtered and unfiltered references. Table <a href="#A1.T4" title="Table 4 ‣ Dataset Statistics ‣ Appendix A Appendix ‣ IdeaBench: Benchmarking Large Language Models for Research Idea Generation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4</span></a> shows the descriptive statistics of the filtered and unfiltered reference papers.</p>
</div>
</section>
<section id="A1.SSx4" class="ltx_subsection">
<h3 class="ltx_title ltx_title_subsection">Extracting Research Idea from a Target Paper</h3>

<figure id="A1.F5" class="ltx_figure"><span class="ltx_inline-block"><svg id="A1.F5.pic1" class="ltx_picture" height="161.63" overflow="visible" version="1.1" viewBox="0 0 700 161.63" width="700"><g style="--ltx-stroke-color:#000000;--ltx-fill-color:#000000;" fill="#000000" stroke="#000000" stroke-width="0.4pt" transform="translate(0,161.63) matrix(1 0 0 -1 0 0)"><g style="--ltx-fill-color:#000000;" fill="#000000" fill-opacity="1.0"><path style="stroke:none" d="M 0 5.91 L 0 155.72 C 0 158.98 2.64 161.63 5.91 161.63 L 694.09 161.63 C 697.36 161.63 700 158.98 700 155.72 L 700 5.91 C 700 2.64 697.36 0 694.09 0 L 5.91 0 C 2.64 0 0 2.64 0 5.91 Z"></path></g><g style="--ltx-fill-color:#D9D9D9;" fill="#D9D9D9" fill-opacity="1.0"><path style="stroke:none" d="M 1.97 5.91 L 1.97 137.52 L 698.03 137.52 L 698.03 5.91 C 698.03 3.73 696.27 1.97 694.09 1.97 L 5.91 1.97 C 3.73 1.97 1.97 3.73 1.97 5.91 Z"></path></g><g fill-opacity="1.0" transform="matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 21.65 146.11)"><foreignObject style="--ltx-fo-width:47.46em;--ltx-fo-height:0.69em;--ltx-fo-depth:0.19em;font-size:10pt;" height="12.3" overflow="visible" transform="matrix(1 0 0 -1 0 9.61)" width="656.71"><span class="ltx_foreignobject_container"><span class="ltx_foreignobject_content">
<span id="A1.F5.pic1.1" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_minipage ltx_align_bottom" style="width:47.46em;">
<span id="A1.F5.pic1.1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.F5.pic1.1.1.1" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-fg-color:#FFFFFF;">Prompt template used to extract the research idea from a given target paper’s abstract.</span></span>
</span></span></span></foreignObject></g><g fill-opacity="1.0" transform="matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 21.65 16.47)"><foreignObject style="--ltx-fo-width:47.46em;--ltx-fo-height:7.89em;--ltx-fo-depth:0.19em;font-size:10pt;" height="111.93" overflow="visible" transform="matrix(1 0 0 -1 0 109.24)" width="656.71"><span class="ltx_foreignobject_container"><span class="ltx_foreignobject_content">
<span id="A1.F5.pic1.2" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_minipage ltx_align_bottom" style="width:47.46em;">
<span id="A1.F5.pic1.2.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.F5.pic1.2.1.1" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-fg-color:#000000;">Write a concise paragraph summarizing the following biomedical paper abstract as if you are proposing your own research idea or hypothesis. Focus on describing the main research idea and provide a high-level summary of the findings without detailed results or specific numerical data. Please begin the paragraph with ”Hypothesis: ” or ”Given that ”. 
<br class="ltx_break"></span></span>
<span id="A1.F5.pic1.2.2" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.F5.pic1.2.2.1" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-fg-color:#000000;">Abstract:
<br class="ltx_break"><code id="A1.F5.pic1.2.2.1.1" class="ltx_verbatim ltx_font_typewriter">{target_paper_abstract}</code>
<br class="ltx_break"></span></span>
<span id="A1.F5.pic1.2.3" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.F5.pic1.2.3.1" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-fg-color:#000000;">Summary:</span></span>
</span></span></span></foreignObject></g></g></svg></span>
<figcaption class="ltx_caption"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 5: </span>Prompt template used to extract a target paper’s research idea.</figcaption>
</figure>
<div id="A1.SSx4.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="A1.SSx4.p1.1" class="ltx_p">To ensure a fair comparison between the research ideas generated by LLMs and those in the target paper when ranking the ideas, we extract the core research idea from the target paper’s abstract using GPT-4o with a specifically designed prompt. Abstracts often contain distracting information, such as detailed results, which may not directly reflect the central research idea and may bias the Insight Score when ranking research ideas. Therefore, we designed a prompt that focuses on summarizing the main research idea in a way that aligns with how our LLM generates ideas. Figure <a href="#A1.F5" title="Figure 5 ‣ Extracting Research Idea from a Target Paper ‣ Appendix A Appendix ‣ IdeaBench: Benchmarking Large Language Models for Research Idea Generation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5</span></a> shows the prompt template. This process enables a fair ranking of the target paper’s idea alongside the LLM generated ideas.</p>
</div>
</section>
<section id="A1.SSx5" class="ltx_subsection">
<h3 class="ltx_title ltx_title_subsection">Effect of the Number of Generated Research Ideas on Insight Score</h3>

<figure id="A1.F6" class="ltx_figure"><img src="/html/2411.02429/assets/n-v-absolute_rank.png" id="A1.F6.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_square" width="698" height="585" alt="Refer to caption">
<figcaption class="ltx_caption ltx_centering"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 6: </span>Effect that the number of generated research ideas has on the target paper’s
Average Absolute Rank.</figcaption>
</figure>
<div id="A1.SSx5.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="A1.SSx5.p1.1" class="ltx_p">We assess how the number of generated research ideas <math id="A1.SSx5.p1.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="n" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>n</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">n</annotation></semantics></math> affects the target paper’s absolute rank using GPT-4o Mini, in the same ablation study setting. Figure <a href="#A1.F6" title="Figure 6 ‣ Effect of the Number of Generated Research Ideas on Insight Score ‣ Appendix A Appendix ‣ IdeaBench: Benchmarking Large Language Models for Research Idea Generation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">6</span></a> shows that as <math id="A1.SSx5.p1.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="n" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>n</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">n</annotation></semantics></math> increases, the target paper is ranked farther in the list for both novelty and feasibility, regardless of reference filtering. Changes in the absolute rank of the target paper will affect the Insight Score.</p>
</div>
<div id="A1.SSx5.p2" class="ltx_para">
<p id="A1.SSx5.p2.1" class="ltx_p">To illustrate the effect varying <math id="A1.SSx5.p2.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="n" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>n</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">n</annotation></semantics></math> has on the Insight Score, consider we acquire the Insight Score for an LLM that generates 3 research ideas for one target paper. If the target paper ranks 3rd, then its Insight Score would be <math id="A1.SSx5.p2.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="0.667" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mn>0.667</mn><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">0.667</annotation></semantics></math>. Now, if we have the same LLM generate 10 research ideas and the target paper ranks 5th, as suggested by Figure
<a href="#A1.F6" title="Figure 6 ‣ Effect of the Number of Generated Research Ideas on Insight Score ‣ Appendix A Appendix ‣ IdeaBench: Benchmarking Large Language Models for Research Idea Generation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">6</span></a>, then its Insight Score would be <math id="A1.SSx5.p2.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="0.4" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mn>0.4</mn><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">0.4</annotation></semantics></math>. Despite using the same LLM, the variation in <math id="A1.SSx5.p2.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="n" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>n</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">n</annotation></semantics></math> causes different Insight Scores.</p>
</div>
<div id="A1.SSx5.p3" class="ltx_para">
<p id="A1.SSx5.p3.1" class="ltx_p">Another effect that <math id="A1.SSx5.p3.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="n" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>n</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">n</annotation></semantics></math> has on the Insight Score is the granularity effect, which arises from the discrete nature of the Insight Score. A larger <math id="A1.SSx5.p3.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="n" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>n</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">n</annotation></semantics></math> allows for a more granular measurement of an LLM’s capability in generating research ideas, meaning that a single shift in ranking position results in a smaller change in the Insight Score compared to a smaller <math id="A1.SSx5.p3.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="n" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>n</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">n</annotation></semantics></math>. For example, consider Case <math id="A1.SSx5.p3.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="n=6" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>6</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">n=6</annotation></semantics></math> and Case <math id="A1.SSx5.p3.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="n=10" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>10</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">n=10</annotation></semantics></math>. If the target paper ranks around the top 50%, that is, <math id="A1.SSx5.p3.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="r_{\text{target}_{i}}|_{q}=4" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msub><mrow><msub><mi>r</mi><msub><mtext>target</mtext><mi>i</mi></msub></msub><mo stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mi>q</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>4</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">r_{\text{target}_{i}}|_{q}=4</annotation></semantics></math> for <math id="A1.SSx5.p3.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="n=6" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>6</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">n=6</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="A1.SSx5.p3.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="r_{\text{target}_{i}}|_{q}=6" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msub><mrow><msub><mi>r</mi><msub><mtext>target</mtext><mi>i</mi></msub></msub><mo stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mi>q</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>6</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">r_{\text{target}_{i}}|_{q}=6</annotation></semantics></math> for <math id="A1.SSx5.p3.m9" class="ltx_Math" alttext="n=10" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>10</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">n=10</annotation></semantics></math>, both will have an Insight Score of <math id="A1.SSx5.p3.m10" class="ltx_Math" alttext="0.5" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mn>0.5</mn><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">0.5</annotation></semantics></math>. However, if the target paper’s ranking position drops by one, then for <math id="A1.SSx5.p3.m11" class="ltx_Math" alttext="n=6" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>6</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">n=6</annotation></semantics></math>, <math id="A1.SSx5.p3.m12" class="ltx_Math" alttext="r_{\text{target}_{i}}|_{q}=5" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msub><mrow><msub><mi>r</mi><msub><mtext>target</mtext><mi>i</mi></msub></msub><mo stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mi>q</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>5</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">r_{\text{target}_{i}}|_{q}=5</annotation></semantics></math>, the Insight Score will increase to <math id="A1.SSx5.p3.m13" class="ltx_Math" alttext="0.667" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mn>0.667</mn><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">0.667</annotation></semantics></math>; whereas for <math id="A1.SSx5.p3.m14" class="ltx_Math" alttext="n=10" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>10</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">n=10</annotation></semantics></math>, <math id="A1.SSx5.p3.m15" class="ltx_Math" alttext="r_{\text{target}_{i}}|_{q}=7" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msub><mrow><msub><mi>r</mi><msub><mtext>target</mtext><mi>i</mi></msub></msub><mo stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mi>q</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>7</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">r_{\text{target}_{i}}|_{q}=7</annotation></semantics></math>, the Insight Score will be <math id="A1.SSx5.p3.m16" class="ltx_Math" alttext="0.6" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mn>0.6</mn><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">0.6</annotation></semantics></math>. This difference does not necessarily indicate that the LLM with an Insight Score of <math id="A1.SSx5.p3.m17" class="ltx_Math" alttext="0.667" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mn>0.667</mn><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">0.667</annotation></semantics></math> is better than the one with <math id="A1.SSx5.p3.m18" class="ltx_Math" alttext="0.6" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mn>0.6</mn><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">0.6</annotation></semantics></math>. Instead, it reflects that the first model generated fewer research ideas, resulting in a less granular Insight Score.
Thus, using the same <math id="A1.SSx5.p3.m19" class="ltx_Math" alttext="n" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>n</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">n</annotation></semantics></math> for comparing different LLMs’ Insight Scores is recommended to avoid unfair comparison.</p>
</div>
</section>
<section id="A1.SSx6" class="ltx_subsection">
<h3 class="ltx_title ltx_title_subsection">Case Studies</h3>

<div id="A1.SSx6.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="A1.SSx6.p1.1" class="ltx_p">In this section, we include a series of case studies that illustrate the capabilities of LLMs in generating research ideas, supporting our main benchmark’s findings. Each case study includes 10 examples showcasing how LLMs generate research ideas that are similar to, more novel than, and comparable in feasibility to their target papers. Additionally, two examples highlight how a smaller LLM produces incoherent and irrelevant text. Through these examples, we show the strengths of LLMs in aligning with target papers and sometimes surpassing them in novelty while maintaining comparable feasibility. We also highlight that smaller models may be incapable of generating coherent and relevant research ideas.</p>
</div>
<section id="A1.SSx6.SSSx1" class="ltx_subsubsection">
<h4 class="ltx_title ltx_title_subsubsection">Generated research ideas similar to their target paper.</h4>

<div id="A1.SSx6.SSSx1.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="A1.SSx6.SSSx1.p1.1" class="ltx_p">We find that LLMs can emulate human researchers by generating research ideas similar to those in their target papers when provided with the same background information. We present examples where LLM-generated research ideas exhibit impressive overlap with their target papers, despite these papers not being seen during the LLMs’ training. These examples are shown in Figures <a href="#A1.F7" title="Figure 7 ‣ LLama 3.1 8B-Instruct results. ‣ Case Studies ‣ Appendix A Appendix ‣ IdeaBench: Benchmarking Large Language Models for Research Idea Generation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">7</span></a> through <a href="#A1.F16" title="Figure 16 ‣ LLama 3.1 8B-Instruct results. ‣ Case Studies ‣ Appendix A Appendix ‣ IdeaBench: Benchmarking Large Language Models for Research Idea Generation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">16</span></a>. Each example includes the target paper’s abstract, the LLM-generated idea, and an explanation of the idea overlap rating, highlighting why the two are very similar. Key points of overlap between the target paper’s abstract and the generated research idea are spotlighted in green by human researchers.</p>
</div>
<div id="A1.SSx6.SSSx1.p2" class="ltx_para">
<p id="A1.SSx6.SSSx1.p2.1" class="ltx_p">Through these examples, we notice that LLMs are capable of identifying and leveraging the most relevant ideas from all the unfiltered references of the target papers, allowing them to generate research ideas that address issues similar to those in the target paper. Additionally, these models can produce ideas that predict findings that are related or remarkably close to those presented in the original work. This suggests that LLMs can not only identify key research questions but also anticipate the outcomes, aligning closely with the conclusions of the target papers.</p>
</div>
</section>
<section id="A1.SSx6.SSSx2" class="ltx_subsubsection">
<h4 class="ltx_title ltx_title_subsubsection">Novelty of the generated research ideas.</h4>

<div id="A1.SSx6.SSSx2.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="A1.SSx6.SSSx2.p1.1" class="ltx_p">Our main results demonstrate that LLMs can generate research ideas that are as novel, if not more so, than those in their target papers. We provide examples where LLM-generated research ideas outrank their target papers in terms of novelty in Figures <a href="#A1.F17" title="Figure 17 ‣ LLama 3.1 8B-Instruct results. ‣ Case Studies ‣ Appendix A Appendix ‣ IdeaBench: Benchmarking Large Language Models for Research Idea Generation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">17</span></a> through <a href="#A1.F26" title="Figure 26 ‣ LLama 3.1 8B-Instruct results. ‣ Case Studies ‣ Appendix A Appendix ‣ IdeaBench: Benchmarking Large Language Models for Research Idea Generation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">26</span></a>. These figures include the target paper’s research idea, the generated research idea, and the Insight Score’s rationale for ranking the generated idea higher in novelty. Human researchers highlight the aspects contributing to the generated idea’s novelty in green and those that illustrate why the target paper’s idea is less novel in red. These examples showcase the capability of LLMs to produce novel research ideas.</p>
</div>
<div id="A1.SSx6.SSSx2.p2" class="ltx_para">
<p id="A1.SSx6.SSSx2.p2.1" class="ltx_p">From these examples, we observe that LLMs generate novel research ideas by creatively bridging connections between different concepts or findings. When the generated research idea is ranked higher in novelty than the target paper’s idea, it is often because the target paper’s idea incrementally builds on existing research, while the LLM-generated ideas propose new connections between different concepts or scientific findings from reference papers. This suggests that given the proper background information LLMs can generate bold and novel research ideas.</p>
</div>
</section>
<section id="A1.SSx6.SSSx3" class="ltx_subsubsection">
<h4 class="ltx_title ltx_title_subsubsection">Feasibility of the generated research ideas.</h4>

<div id="A1.SSx6.SSSx3.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="A1.SSx6.SSSx3.p1.1" class="ltx_p">Although our results show that LLMs often don’t generate research ideas that are more feasible than those in their target papers, there are still some instances where LLMs’ research ideas are comparable in feasibility to their target papers’ ideas.
We provide examples where LLM-generated research ideas are comparable in feasibility to their target papers’ ideas in
Figures <a href="#A1.F27" title="Figure 27 ‣ LLama 3.1 8B-Instruct results. ‣ Case Studies ‣ Appendix A Appendix ‣ IdeaBench: Benchmarking Large Language Models for Research Idea Generation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">27</span></a> through <a href="#A1.F36" title="Figure 36 ‣ LLama 3.1 8B-Instruct results. ‣ Case Studies ‣ Appendix A Appendix ‣ IdeaBench: Benchmarking Large Language Models for Research Idea Generation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">36</span></a>. These figures include the target paper’s research idea, the generated research idea, and the Insight Score’s rationale for the generated idea’s feasibility. Human researchers highlight the elements that contribute to the generated idea’s feasibility in green. These examples showcase the LLMs’ ability to produce feasible research ideas. By analyzing these examples, we observe that when LLMs generate feasible research ideas, the ideas are generally straightforward and rely on established technologies and approaches.</p>
</div>
</section>
<section id="A1.SSx6.SSSx4" class="ltx_subsubsection">
<h4 class="ltx_title ltx_title_subsubsection">LLama 3.1 8B-Instruct results.</h4>

<div id="A1.SSx6.SSSx4.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="A1.SSx6.SSSx4.p1.1" class="ltx_p">In order to evaluate the effectiveness of small LLMs in generating research ideas, we tested LLama 3.1 8B-Instruct in both low-resource and high-resource conditions. The results are presented in Table <a href="#A1.T5" title="Table 5 ‣ LLama 3.1 8B-Instruct results. ‣ Case Studies ‣ Appendix A Appendix ‣ IdeaBench: Benchmarking Large Language Models for Research Idea Generation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5</span></a>. LLama 3.1 8B-Instruct reports a high idea overlap and novelty Insight Score but a low feasibility Insight Score due to generating large amounts of irrelevant or incoherent text. Our LLM-based evaluations (idea overlap and Insight Score) are not equipped to manage this because LLMs are not trained on poor quality text and our prompt templates do not consider incoherent text. As a result, the irrelevant and bad text introduces bias in our evaluations. Examples of irrelevant and incoherent text generated by LLama 3.1 8B-Instruct can be seen in Figures <a href="#A1.F37" title="Figure 37 ‣ LLama 3.1 8B-Instruct results. ‣ Case Studies ‣ Appendix A Appendix ‣ IdeaBench: Benchmarking Large Language Models for Research Idea Generation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">37</span></a> and <a href="#A1.F38" title="Figure 38 ‣ LLama 3.1 8B-Instruct results. ‣ Case Studies ‣ Appendix A Appendix ‣ IdeaBench: Benchmarking Large Language Models for Research Idea Generation" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">38</span></a>, where sections highlighted in red by human researchers indicate problematic text produced by the LLM.</p>
</div>
<div id="A1.SSx6.SSSx4.p2" class="ltx_para">
<p id="A1.SSx6.SSSx4.p2.1" class="ltx_p">Since LLama 3.1 8B-Instruct generates a significant amount of irrelevant and nonsensical text, and our LLM evaluations are not suitable for these outputs, we opted not to include its results in the main findings. We present these outcomes to inform the community that smaller LLMs are insufficient for producing coherent research ideas.</p>
</div>
<figure id="A1.F7" class="ltx_figure">
<div id="A1.F7.1" class="ltx_logical-block ltx_minipage ltx_align_middle" style="width:455.3pt;">

<span id="A1.F7.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2.1" class="ltx_p">Large-scale sequence modeling has sparked rapid advances that now extend into biology and genomics. However, <span id="A1.F7.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2.1.1" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-bg-color:#CCFFCC;">modeling genomic sequences introduces challenges such as the need to model long-range token interactions, the effects of upstream and downstream regions of the genome, and the reverse complementarity (RC) of DNA.</span>Here, we propose an architecture motivated by these challenges that <span id="A1.F7.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2.1.2" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-bg-color:#CCFFCC;">builds off the long-range Mamba block, and extends it to a BiMamba component that supports bi-directionality, and to a MambaDNA block that additionally supports RC equivariance.</span> We use MambaDNA as the basis of Caduceus, the first family of RC equivariant bi-directional long-range DNA language models, and we introduce pre-training and fine-tuning strategies that yield Caduceus DNA foundation models. <span id="A1.F7.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2.1.3" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-bg-color:#CCFFCC;">Caduceus outperforms previous long-range models on downstream benchmarks; on a challenging long-range variant effect prediction task,</span> Caduceus exceeds the performance of 10x larger models that do not leverage bi-directionality or equivariance.</span>

<span id="A1.F8.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.1" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_minipage ltx_align_bottom" style="width:34.15em;">
<span id="A1.F8.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.F8.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold" style="--ltx-fg-color:#FFFFFF;">Target Paper Abstract</span></span>
</span></span></span></foreignObject></g><g fill-opacity="1.0" transform="matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 21.65 16.47)"><foreignObject style="--ltx-fo-width:39.27em;--ltx-fo-height:17.49em;--ltx-fo-depth:0.19em;font-size:10pt;" height="244.76" overflow="visible" transform="matrix(1 0 0 -1 0 242.07)" width="543.39"><span class="ltx_foreignobject_container"><span class="ltx_foreignobject_content">
<span id="A1.F8.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_minipage ltx_align_bottom" style="width:39.27em;">
<span id="A1.F8.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2.1" class="ltx_p">Traumatic brain injuries represent a leading cause of death and disability in the paediatric and adult populations. Moderate-to-severe injuries are associated with blood–brain barrier dysfunction, the development of cerebral oedema, and neuroinflammation. <span id="A1.F8.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2.1.1" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-bg-color:#CCFFCC;">Antagonists of the tachykinin NK1 receptor have been proposed as potential agents for the post-injury treatment of TBI.</span> We report on the identification of EUC-001 as a potential clinical candidate for development as a novel TBI therapy. EUC-001 is a selective NK1 antagonist with a high affinity for the human NK1 receptor (Ki 5.75 × 10-10 M). It has sufficient aqueous solubility to enable intravenous administration, whilst still retaining good CNS penetration as evidenced by its ability to inhibit the gerbil foot-tapping response. <span id="A1.F8.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2.1.2" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-bg-color:#CCFFCC;">Using an animal model of TBI, the post-injury administration of EUC-001 was shown to restore BBB function in a dose-dependent manner.EUC-001 was also able to ameliorate cerebral oedema.</span> These effects were associated with a significant reduction in post-TBI mortality. In addition, EUC-001 was able to significantly reduce functional deficits, both motor and cognitive, that normally follow a severe injury. EUC-001 is proposed as an ideal candidate for clinical development for TBI.</span>
</span></span></span></foreignObject></g></g></svg></span>

<span id="A1.F9.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.F9.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold" style="--ltx-fg-color:#FFFFFF;">Target Paper Abstract</span></span>
</span></span></span></foreignObject></g><g fill-opacity="1.0" transform="matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 21.65 16.47)"><foreignObject style="--ltx-fo-width:39.27em;--ltx-fo-height:24.75em;--ltx-fo-depth:0.19em;font-size:10pt;" height="345.16" overflow="visible" transform="matrix(1 0 0 -1 0 342.47)" width="543.39"><span class="ltx_foreignobject_container"><span class="ltx_foreignobject_content">
<span id="A1.F9.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_minipage ltx_align_bottom" style="width:39.27em;">
<span id="A1.F9.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2.1" class="ltx_p">Patients with coronavirus disease-2019 (COVID-19) have an increased risk of thrombosis and acute respiratory distress syndrome (ARDS). Thrombosis is often attributed to increases in plasminogen activator inhibitor-1 (PAI-1) and a shut-down of fibrinolysis (blood clot dissolution). <span id="A1.F9.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2.1.1" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-bg-color:#CCFFCC;">Decreased urokinase-type plasminogen activator (uPA), a protease necessary for cell-associated plasmin generation, and increased tissue-type plasminogen activator (tPA) and PAI-1 levels have been reported in COVID-19 patients.</span> Because these factors can occur in free and complexed forms with differences in their biological functions, we examined the predictive impact of uPA, tPA, and PAI-1 in their free forms and complexes as a biomarker for COVID-19 severity and the development of ARDS. In this retrospective study of 69 Japanese adults hospitalized with COVID-19 and 20 healthy donors, <span id="A1.F9.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2.1.2" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-bg-color:#CCFFCC;">we found elevated free, non-complexed PAI-1 antigen, low circulating uPA, and uPA/PAI-1 but not tPA/PAI-1 complex levels to be associated with COVID-19 severity and ARDS development.</span> This biomarker profile was typical for patients in the complicated phase. Lack of PAI-1 activity in circulation despite free, non-complexed PAI-1 protein and plasmin/<math id="A1.F9.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\alpha" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>α</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\alpha</annotation></semantics></math>2anti-plasmin complex correlated with suPAR and sVCAM levels, markers indicating endothelial dysfunction. Furthermore, uPA/PAI-1 complex levels positively correlated with TNF<math id="A1.F9.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\alpha" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>α</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\alpha</annotation></semantics></math>, a cytokine reported to trigger inflammatory cell death and tissue damage. Those levels also positively correlated with lymphopenia and the pro-inflammatory factors interleukin1<math id="A1.F9.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\beta" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>β</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\beta</annotation></semantics></math> (IL1<math id="A1.F9.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\beta" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>β</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\beta</annotation></semantics></math>), IL6, and C-reactive protein, markers associated with the anti-viral inflammatory response. These findings argue for using uPA and uPA/PAI-1 as novel biomarkers to detect patients at risk of developing severe COVID-19, including ARDS.</span>

<span id="A1.F9.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.4" class="ltx_p">Hypothesis: <span id="A1.F9.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.4.1" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-bg-color:#CCFFCC;">The dysregulation of the plasminogen/plasmin system, particularly involving elevated levels of plasminogen activator inhibitor-1 (PAI-1) and urokinase plasminogen activator (uPA), plays a crucial role in the exacerbation of COVID-19-related coagulopathy and inflammation,</span> particularly in diverse ethnic groups. <span id="A1.F10.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.1" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_minipage ltx_align_bottom" style="width:34.15em;">
<span id="A1.F10.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.F10.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold" style="--ltx-fg-color:#FFFFFF;">Target Paper Abstract</span></span>
</span></span></span></foreignObject></g><g fill-opacity="1.0" transform="matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 21.65 16.47)"><foreignObject style="--ltx-fo-width:39.27em;--ltx-fo-height:21.15em;--ltx-fo-depth:0.19em;font-size:10pt;" height="295.35" overflow="visible" transform="matrix(1 0 0 -1 0 292.66)" width="543.39"><span class="ltx_foreignobject_container"><span class="ltx_foreignobject_content">
<span id="A1.F10.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_minipage ltx_align_bottom" style="width:39.27em;">
<span id="A1.F10.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2.1" class="ltx_p">Increased inflammation is associated with the pathogenesis of heart failure (HF). Increased circulating levels of cytokines have been previously reported and generally associated with worse clinical outcomes. In this context, the modulation of inflammation-related parameters seems to be a reasonable therapeutic option for improving the clinical course of the disease. Based on this, we aimed to compare changes in circulating cytokines when Mediterranean diet alone or in combination with hypercaloric, hyperproteic oral nutritional supplements (ONS), enriched with omega-3 (n-3) polyunsaturated fatty acids were administered to patients with HF. <span id="A1.F10.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2.1.1" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-bg-color:#CCFFCC;">Briefly, patients were randomly assigned to receive Mediterranean Diet (control group) vs. Mediterranean Diet plus ONS (intervention group). We observed increased circulating levels of IL-6, IL-8, MCP-1 and IP-10. MCP-1 and IL-6 were associated with overweight and obesity (p = 0.01-0.01-0.04, respectively); IL-6 and IL-8 were positively correlated with fat mass and CRP serum levels (p = 0.02-0.04, respectively).</span> Circulating levels of IL-8 significantly decreased in all patients treated with the Mediterranean diet, while IL-6 and IP-10 only significantly decreased in patients that received plus ONS. In the univariate analysis, MCP-1 and its combination with IL-6 were associated with increased mortality (p = 0.02), while the multivariate analysis confirmed that MCP-1 was an independent factor for mortality (OR 1.01, 95%ci 1.01-1.02). In conclusion, nutritional support using hypercaloric, hyperproteic, n-3 enriched ONS in combination with Mediterranean Diet was associated with decreased circulating levels of some cytokines and <span id="A1.F10.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2.1.2" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-bg-color:#CCFFCC;">could represent an interesting step for improving heart functionality of patients with HF</span>.</span>
</span></span></span></foreignObject></g></g></svg></span>

<span id="A1.F10.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_minipage ltx_align_bottom" style="width:39.27em;">
<span id="A1.F10.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.F10.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">LLM:</span> GPT-4o Mini</span>
<span id="A1.F10.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.2" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.F10.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Resource Scenario:</span> High</span>
<span id="A1.F10.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.3" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.F10.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.3.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Generated Research idea:</span></span>
<span id="A1.F10.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.4" class="ltx_p">Given the strong interconnections between malnutrition, sarcopenia, inflammation, and cardiovascular health highlighted across several studies, a novel hypothesis could be formulated around the <span id="A1.F10.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.4.1" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-bg-color:#CCFFCC;">role of targeted nutritional interventions</span> in modulating inflammatory responses and improving muscle mass and functionality in patients with heart failure (HF) and concurrent sarcopenia. <span id="A1.F10.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.4.2" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-bg-color:#CCFFCC;">Specifically, we propose that a combined supplementation of omega-3 polyunsaturated fatty acids (PUFAs) and hypercaloric, hyperproteic oral supplements enriched with leucine will significantly reduce inflammatory markers (such as IL-6 and MCP-1) while enhancing muscle mass and strength in individuals with HF and sarcopenia. This effect may subsequently improve overall cardiovascular outcomes</span>, including left ventricular function and quality of life, by addressing both the nutritional deficits and the inflammatory milieu that contribute to the progression of heart failure in this population. Future studies could test this hypothesis to explore the synergistic effects of these interventions on both muscle health and inflammation.</span>

</span></span></span></foreignObject></g><g fill-opacity="1.0" transform="matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 21.65 16.47)"><foreignObject style="--ltx-fo-width:39.27em;--ltx-fo-height:13.89em;--ltx-fo-depth:0.19em;font-size:10pt;" height="194.95" overflow="visible" transform="matrix(1 0 0 -1 0 192.26)" width="543.39"><span class="ltx_foreignobject_container"><span class="ltx_foreignobject_content">
<span id="A1.F11.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_minipage ltx_align_bottom" style="width:39.27em;">
<span id="A1.F11.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2.1" class="ltx_p">The Anthropocene signifies the start of a no-analogue trajectory of the Earth system that is fundamentally different from the Holocene. This new trajectory is characterized by rising risks of triggering irreversible and unmanageable shifts in Earth system functioning. <span id="A1.F11.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2.1.1" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-bg-color:#CCFFCC;">We urgently need a new global approach to safeguard critical Earth system regulating functions more effectively and comprehensively. The global commons framework is the closest example of an existing approach with the aim of governing biophysical systems on Earth upon which the world collectively depends. Derived during stable Holocene conditions, the global commons framework must now evolve in the light of new Anthropocene dynamics</span>. This requires a fundamental shift from a focus only on governing shared resources beyond national jurisdiction, to one that secures critical functions of the Earth system irrespective of national boundaries. We propose a new framework—the planetary commons—which differs from the global commons framework by including not only globally shared geographic regions but also critical biophysical systems that regulate the resilience and state, and therefore livability, on Earth. <span id="A1.F11.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2.1.2" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-bg-color:#CCFFCC;">The new planetary commons should articulate and create comprehensive stewardship obligations through Earth system governance aimed at restoring and strengthening planetary resilience and justice</span>.</span>

<span id="A1.F11.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.F11.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">LLM:</span> GPT-3.5 Turbo</span>
<span id="A1.F11.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.2" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.F11.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Resource Scenario:</span> High</span>
<span id="A1.F11.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.3" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.F11.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.3.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Generated Research idea:</span>

<span id="A1.F13.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.F13.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2.1.1" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-bg-color:#CCFFCC;">Salt stress profoundly affects plant growth, prompting intricate molecular responses, such as alternative splicing (AS), for environmental adaptation.</span> However, the response of AS events to salt stress in Opisthopappus (Opisthopappus taihangensis and Opisthopappus longilobus) remains unclear, which is a Taihang Mountain cliff-dwelling species. Using RNA-seq data, differentially expressed genes (DEGs) were identified under time and concentration gradients of salt stress. Two types of AS, skipped exon (SE) and mutually exclusive exons (MXE), were found. Differentially alternative splicing (DAS) genes in both species were significantly enriched in “protein phosphorylation”, “starch and sucrose metabolism”, and “plant hormone signal transduction” pathways. Meanwhile, distinct GO terms and KEGG pathways of DAS occurred between two species. Only a small subset of DAS genes overlapped with DEGs under salt stress. <span id="A1.F13.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2.1.2" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-bg-color:#CCFFCC;">Although both species likely adopted protein phosphorylation to enhance salt stress tolerance, they exhibited distinct responses. The results indicated that the salt stress mechanisms of both Opisthopappus species exhibited similarities and differences in response to salt stress, which suggested that adaptive divergence might have occurred between them.</span> This study initially provides a comprehensive description of salt responsive AS events in Opisthopappus and conveys some insights into the molecular mechanisms behind species tolerance on the Taihang Mountains.</span>

<span id="A1.F14.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2.1" class="ltx_p">A role for vitamin D in immune modulation and in cancer has been suggested. In this work, we report that mice with increased availability of vitamin D display greater immune-dependent resistance to transplantable cancers and augmented responses to checkpoint blockade immunotherapies. Similarly, in humans, vitamin D–induced genes correlate with improved responses to immune checkpoint inhibitor treatment as well as with immunity to cancer and increased overall survival. <span id="A1.F14.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2.1.1" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-bg-color:#CCFFCC;">In mice, resistance is attributable to the activity of vitamin D on intestinal epithelial cells, which alters microbiome composition in favor of Bacteroides fragilis, which positively regulates cancer immunity. Our findings indicate a previously unappreciated connection between vitamin D, microbial commensal communities, and immune responses to cancer. Collectively, they highlight vitamin D levels as a potential determinant of cancer immunity and immunotherapy success.</span> Editor’s summary The gut microbiome has been shown to modulate the response of cancer patients to therapy, but precisely how microbiota affect anticancer immunity is still being elucidated. Giampazolias et al. report that vitamin D bioavailability in mice influences the composition of the gut microbiome (see the Perspective by Franco and McCoy). After dietary manipulation, vitamin D levels were observed to affect gut bacteria, which in turn improved cancer immunotherapy and antitumor immunity. In humans, low vitamin D levels were correlated with tumor development, and gene signatures of vitamin D activity were associated with improved patient responses to immunotherapy. These findings highlight the connection between vitamin D and the immune system through gut bacteria and may have applications for improving cancer therapies. —Priscilla N. Kelly</span>

</span></span></span></foreignObject></g><g fill-opacity="1.0" transform="matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 21.65 16.47)"><foreignObject style="--ltx-fo-width:39.27em;--ltx-fo-height:15.15em;--ltx-fo-depth:0.19em;font-size:10pt;" height="212.32" overflow="visible" transform="matrix(1 0 0 -1 0 209.63)" width="543.39"><span class="ltx_foreignobject_container"><span class="ltx_foreignobject_content">
<span id="A1.F15.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_minipage ltx_align_bottom" style="width:39.27em;">
<span id="A1.F15.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2.1" class="ltx_p">Messenger RNA (mRNA) vaccines were highly effective against the ancestral SARS-CoV-2 strain, but the efficacy of bivalent mRNA boosters against XBB variants was substantially lower. <span id="A1.F15.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2.1.1" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-bg-color:#CCFFCC;">Here, we show limited durability of neutralizing antibody (NAb) responses against XBB variants and isotype switching to immunoglobulin G4 (IgG4) responses following bivalent mRNA boosting.</span> Bivalent mRNA boosting elicited modest XBB.1-, XBB.1.5-, and XBB.1.16-specific NAbs that waned rapidly within 3 months. In contrast, bivalent mRNA boosting induced more robust and sustained NAbs against the ancestral WA1/2020 strain, suggesting immune imprinting. Following bivalent mRNA boosting, serum antibody responses were primarily IgG2 and IgG4 responses with poor Fc functional activity. In contrast, a third monovalent mRNA immunization boosted all isotypes including IgG1 and IgG3 with robust Fc functional activity. These data show substantial immune imprinting for the ancestral spike and isotype switching to IgG4 responses following bivalent mRNA boosting, with important implications for future booster designs and boosting strategies.</span>

<span id="A1.F18.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.F18.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold" style="--ltx-fg-color:#FFFFFF;">Target Paper Research Idea</span></span>
</span></span></span></foreignObject></g><g fill-opacity="1.0" transform="matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 21.65 16.47)"><foreignObject style="--ltx-fo-width:39.27em;--ltx-fo-height:11.49em;--ltx-fo-depth:0.19em;font-size:10pt;" height="161.74" overflow="visible" transform="matrix(1 0 0 -1 0 159.05)" width="543.39"><span class="ltx_foreignobject_container"><span class="ltx_foreignobject_content">
<span id="A1.F18.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_minipage ltx_align_bottom" style="width:39.27em;">
<span id="A1.F18.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2.1" class="ltx_p">Given that the increasing aging population significantly strains public medical resources, exploring efficient home health care (HHC) solutions becomes critical. This research proposes addressing the service resource routing and scheduling problem by optimizing the sharing strategy among multiple HHC centers. The study aims to minimize both total operation costs and service delays. <span id="A1.F18.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2.1.1" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-bg-color:#FFCCCC;">A mixed integer programming model is used to describe the problem, and a multi-objective artificial bee colony algorithm with problem-specific knowledge (MABC-PK) is developed to find solutions.</span>The algorithm incorporates problem-specific heuristics, a crossover operation, a self-learning neighborhood selection method, and local search techniques to refine solutions. Comparative experiments demonstrate that MABC-PK outperforms several established multi-objective optimization methods, highlighting its effectiveness for this application.</span>
</span></span></span></foreignObject></g></g></svg></span>

<span id="A1.F18.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.2" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.F18.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Resource Scenario:</span> High</span>
<span id="A1.F18.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.3" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.F18.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.3.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Generated Research idea:</span> Based on the insights gleaned from the abstracts, I propose the following novel hypothesis:”<span id="A1.F18.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.3.2" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-bg-color:#CCFFCC;">Integrating home healthcare logistics with smart city transportation systems using autonomous vehicles can significantly enhance the efficiency of caregiver routing and scheduling. By leveraging real-time traffic data and predictive analytics, an intelligent system can dynamically optimize routes, reduce travel times, and balance workloads among caregivers</span>. This approach aims to minimize operational costs while maximizing patient satisfaction and care continuity. The hypothesis can be tested by developing a hybrid optimization algorithm combining elements of vehicle routing problem (VRP), multi-objective optimization, and machine learning, and applying it to real-world home healthcare scenarios in urban environments.”This hypothesis builds on the concepts of vehicle routing, scheduling optimization, autonomous vehicle coordination, and home healthcare logistics, while proposing a novel integration of these domains to address the unique challenges faced in urban home healthcare services.</span>

<span id="A1.F19.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.F19.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold" style="--ltx-fg-color:#FFFFFF;">Target Paper Research Idea</span></span>
</span></span></span></foreignObject></g><g fill-opacity="1.0" transform="matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 21.65 13.78)"><foreignObject style="--ltx-fo-width:39.27em;--ltx-fo-height:15.09em;--ltx-fo-depth:0em;font-size:10pt;" height="208.86" overflow="visible" transform="matrix(1 0 0 -1 0 208.86)" width="543.39"><span class="ltx_foreignobject_container"><span class="ltx_foreignobject_content">
<span id="A1.F19.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_minipage ltx_align_bottom" style="width:39.27em;">
<span id="A1.F19.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2.1" class="ltx_p">Given that addressing sensor node and route failures in smart wireless livestock sensor networks is critical for maintaining network performance, the proposed research will investigate the time-aware UAV-based energy-efficient reconfigurable routing (TUBER) scheme. TUBER aims to mitigate the time complexity and processing cost issues noted in previous schemes by implementing a synchronized clustering-with-backup strategy, a minimum-hop neighborhood recovery mechanism, and a redundancy minimization technique. <span id="A1.F19.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2.1.1" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-bg-color:#FFCCCC;">Comparative evaluations indicate that TUBER outperforms earlier schemes such as RUBER and UBER across several network performance metrics, </span>demonstrating significant improvements in areas including cluster survival ratio, network stability, energy dissipation, network coverage, packet delivery, fault tolerance, load balancing, routing overhead, routing delay, and failure recovery. These findings suggest that TUBER could provide a more robust and efficient solution for managing sensor node and route failures in these networks.</span>

<span id="A1.F20.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_minipage ltx_align_bottom" style="width:39.27em;">
<span id="A1.F20.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2.1" class="ltx_p">Given that anesthetics impact brain regions differently depending on sex, our research aims to <span id="A1.F20.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2.1.1" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-bg-color:#FFCCCC;">investigate the role of sex hormones in modulating anesthetic sensitivity</span>. This study reveals that females, both in mice and humans, exhibit resistance to the hypnotic effects of volatile anesthetics primarily due to the acute influence of sex hormones. Although these behavioral differences do not manifest in standard cortical electroencephalographic recordings used clinically, they are evident in subcortical activity patterns. This hidden resistance might contribute to the higher incidence of awareness during anesthesia in females, suggesting a need for sex-specific anesthetic protocols.</span>

<span id="A1.F20.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.3" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.F20.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.3.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Generated Research idea:</span></span>
<span id="A1.F20.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.4" class="ltx_p">Based on the interplay between neural mechanisms underlying anesthesia, arousal states, and cognitive function detailed across these abstracts, I propose a novel hypothesis: ”<span id="A1.F20.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.4.1" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-bg-color:#CCFFCC;">The differential activation and inhibition of sleep-promoting and wake-active neuronal populations within the preoptic area (POA) and their interaction with the orexinergic system are critical determinants of individual variability in cognitive recovery trajectories and the incidence of postoperative cognitive dysfunction (POCD) following general anesthesia. Specifically, dysregulation of these pathways may result in protracted neurocognitive recovery and increased susceptibility to POCD, particularly in older adults or those with preexisting neurocognitive vulnerabilities.</span>” This hypothesis integrates the molecular and cellular insights about POA and orexinergic neurons with the variability in anesthesia recovery patterns, aiming to elucidate the mechanisms behind cognitive resilience and dysfunction post-anesthesia.</span>

<span id="A1.F21.p1.pic1.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent"><span class="ltx_inline-block"><svg id="A1.F21.p1.pic1.p1.pic1" class="ltx_picture" color="#000000" height="192.15" overflow="visible" version="1.1" viewBox="0 0 586.69 192.15" width="586.69"><g style="--ltx-stroke-color:#000000;--ltx-fill-color:#000000;" fill="#000000" stroke="#000000" stroke-width="0.4pt" transform="translate(0,192.15) matrix(1 0 0 -1 0 0)"><g style="--ltx-fill-color:#000000;" fill="#000000" fill-opacity="1.0"><path style="stroke:none" d="M 0 5.91 L 0 186.24 C 0 189.5 2.64 192.15 5.91 192.15 L 580.78 192.15 C 584.04 192.15 586.69 189.5 586.69 186.24 L 586.69 5.91 C 586.69 2.64 584.04 0 580.78 0 L 5.91 0 C 2.64 0 0 2.64 0 5.91 Z"></path></g><g style="--ltx-fill-color:#FFFFFF;" fill="#FFFFFF" fill-opacity="1.0"><path style="stroke:none" d="M 1.97 5.91 L 1.97 168.03 L 584.72 168.03 L 584.72 5.91 C 584.72 3.73 582.96 1.97 580.78 1.97 L 5.91 1.97 C 3.73 1.97 1.97 3.73 1.97 5.91 Z"></path></g><g fill-opacity="1.0" transform="matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 21.65 176.63)"><foreignObject style="--ltx-fo-width:34.15em;--ltx-fo-height:0.69em;--ltx-fo-depth:0.19em;font-size:10pt;" height="12.3" overflow="visible" transform="matrix(1 0 0 -1 0 9.61)" width="472.54"><span class="ltx_foreignobject_container"><span class="ltx_foreignobject_content">

<span id="A1.F21.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.F21.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold" style="--ltx-fg-color:#FFFFFF;">Target Paper Research Idea</span></span>
</span></span></span></foreignObject></g><g fill-opacity="1.0" transform="matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 21.65 13.78)"><foreignObject style="--ltx-fo-width:39.27em;--ltx-fo-height:10.29em;--ltx-fo-depth:0em;font-size:10pt;" height="142.45" overflow="visible" transform="matrix(1 0 0 -1 0 142.45)" width="543.39"><span class="ltx_foreignobject_container"><span class="ltx_foreignobject_content">
<span id="A1.F21.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_minipage ltx_align_bottom" style="width:39.27em;">
<span id="A1.F21.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2.1" class="ltx_p">Given that detailed knowledge of synaptic circuitry in the human brain is essential for understanding brain function, this study by Shapson-Coe et al. leverages human brain tissue obtained from neurosurgical procedures to achieve a high-resolution reconstruction of a cubic millimeter of the human temporal cortex. <span id="A1.F21.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2.1.1" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-bg-color:#FFCCCC;">Utilizing advanced electron microscopy and computational techniques, the study generated an extensive dataset encompassing thousands of neurons, millions of synapses, and various other brain tissue elements.</span> Key findings include the identification of a new class of directionally oriented neurons in deep cortical layers and the discovery of rare, powerful multisynaptic connections. The freely available dataset and analysis tools provided by the authors offer a valuable resource for future research into both normal and disordered human neural circuits.</span>
</span></span></span></foreignObject></g></g></svg></span>

<span id="A1.F21.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.F21.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">LLM:</span> Llama 3.1 405B-Instruct</span>
<span id="A1.F21.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.2" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.F21.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Resource Scenario:</span> High</span>
<span id="A1.F21.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.3" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.F21.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.3.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Generated Research idea:</span> The following is a novel hypothesis inspired by the abstracts provided: The human brain’s ability to reorganize and adapt throughout life may be influenced by the dynamic interplay between astrocytes and neurons. Specifically, the territorial organization of astrocytes and their interactions with neurons may play a crucial role in modulating synaptic plasticity and neural circuitry. <span id="A1.F21.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.3.2" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-bg-color:#CCFFCC;">I propose that astrocytes act as ’synaptic editors,’ selectively pruning or strengthening synapses based on neural activity patterns, thereby shaping the brain’s neural networks and influencing cognitive function. Furthermore, disruptions in this astrocyte-neuron interplay may contribute to neurological disorders such as schizophrenia, where abnormalities in neural circuitry and synaptic function have been observed.</span> Investigating the mechanisms underlying astrocyte-mediated synaptic editing could provide new insights into brain development, plasticity, and disease.” This hypothesis draws inspiration from various abstracts, including those discussing astrocyte-neuron interactions (Abstracts 8, 29), synaptic plasticity and neural circuitry (Abstracts 6, 10, 11), and neurological disorders such as schizophrenia (Abstract 33). The idea of astrocytes as ’synaptic editors’ is a novel concept that integrates these different lines of research and provides a new perspective on the complex interactions between astrocytes and neurons in the brain.’</span>

<span id="A1.F21.p1.pic1.p3.pic1.2.1" class="ltx_p">1. **Hypothesis C**: The idea of astrocytes acting as ’synaptic editors’ is highly novel. <span id="A1.F21.p1.pic1.p3.pic1.2.1.1" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-bg-color:#CCFFCC;">This hypothesis integrates multiple lines of research to propose a new role for astrocytes in modulating synaptic plasticity and neural circuitry, as well as their potential involvement in neurological disorders. This concept of astrocytes selectively pruning or strengthening synapses based on neural activity is a fresh perspective that could lead to significant advancements in understanding brain development and function.</span> … 4. **Hypothesis A**: Although this study by Shapson-Coe et al. offers a high-resolution reconstruction of the human temporal cortex and identifies new classes of neurons and synaptic connections, <span id="A1.F21.p1.pic1.p3.pic1.2.1.2" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-bg-color:#FFCCCC;">it is more about providing a detailed dataset and findings rather than proposing a novel hypothesis.</span> The discovery of new neuronal structures and connections is valuable, but it does not introduce a new conceptual framework like the other hypotheses.</span>

<span id="A1.F22.p1.pic1.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent"><span class="ltx_inline-block"><svg id="A1.F22.p1.pic1.p1.pic1" class="ltx_picture" color="#000000" height="241.96" overflow="visible" version="1.1" viewBox="0 0 586.69 241.96" width="586.69"><g style="--ltx-stroke-color:#000000;--ltx-fill-color:#000000;" fill="#000000" stroke="#000000" stroke-width="0.4pt" transform="translate(0,241.96) matrix(1 0 0 -1 0 0)"><g style="--ltx-fill-color:#000000;" fill="#000000" fill-opacity="1.0"><path style="stroke:none" d="M 0 5.91 L 0 236.05 C 0 239.31 2.64 241.96 5.91 241.96 L 580.78 241.96 C 584.04 241.96 586.69 239.31 586.69 236.05 L 586.69 5.91 C 586.69 2.64 584.04 0 580.78 0 L 5.91 0 C 2.64 0 0 2.64 0 5.91 Z"></path></g><g style="--ltx-fill-color:#FFFFFF;" fill="#FFFFFF" fill-opacity="1.0"><path style="stroke:none" d="M 1.97 5.91 L 1.97 217.85 L 584.72 217.85 L 584.72 5.91 C 584.72 3.73 582.96 1.97 580.78 1.97 L 5.91 1.97 C 3.73 1.97 1.97 3.73 1.97 5.91 Z"></path></g><g fill-opacity="1.0" transform="matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 21.65 226.44)"><foreignObject style="--ltx-fo-width:34.15em;--ltx-fo-height:0.69em;--ltx-fo-depth:0.19em;font-size:10pt;" height="12.3" overflow="visible" transform="matrix(1 0 0 -1 0 9.61)" width="472.54"><span class="ltx_foreignobject_container"><span class="ltx_foreignobject_content">
<span id="A1.F22.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.1" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_minipage ltx_align_bottom" style="width:34.15em;">
<span id="A1.F22.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.F22.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold" style="--ltx-fg-color:#FFFFFF;">Target Paper Research Idea</span></span>
</span></span></span></foreignObject></g><g fill-opacity="1.0" transform="matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 21.65 13.78)"><foreignObject style="--ltx-fo-width:39.27em;--ltx-fo-height:13.89em;--ltx-fo-depth:0em;font-size:10pt;" height="192.26" overflow="visible" transform="matrix(1 0 0 -1 0 192.26)" width="543.39"><span class="ltx_foreignobject_container"><span class="ltx_foreignobject_content">
<span id="A1.F22.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_minipage ltx_align_bottom" style="width:39.27em;">
<span id="A1.F22.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2.1" class="ltx_p">Given that the dual public health emergencies of overdose and COVID-19 have exacerbated risks for individuals with opioid or stimulant use disorders, this study investigates the impact of Risk Mitigation Guidance (RMG) dispensations on mortality and acute care visits. Conducted as a <span id="A1.F22.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2.1.1" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-bg-color:#FFCCCC;">population-based retrospective cohort study in British Columbia, Canada, the research analyzed outcomes for 5882 participants who received RMG prescriptions between March 2020 and August 2021</span>. The findings suggest that RMG opioid dispensations are significantly associated with reduced all-cause and overdose-related mortality, particularly with increased duration of dispensation. However, RMG stimulant dispensations did not show a significant impact on mortality, although they were linked to reduced acute care visits for any cause. This indicates that providing pharmaceutical alternatives to the illegal drug supply might be an effective intervention to lower mortality rates among individuals with opioid use disorder.</span>
</span></span></span></foreignObject></g></g></svg></span>
</span>
<span id="A1.F22.p1.pic1.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent"><span class="ltx_inline-block"><svg id="A1.F22.p1.pic1.p2.pic1" class="ltx_picture" color="#000000" height="288.97" overflow="visible" version="1.1" viewBox="0 0 586.69 288.97" width="586.69"><g style="--ltx-stroke-color:#000000;--ltx-fill-color:#000000;" fill="#000000" stroke="#000000" stroke-width="0.4pt" transform="translate(0,288.97) matrix(1 0 0 -1 0 0)"><g style="--ltx-fill-color:#000000;" fill="#000000" fill-opacity="1.0"><path style="stroke:none" d="M 0 5.91 L 0 283.06 C 0 286.32 2.64 288.97 5.91 288.97 L 580.78 288.97 C 584.04 288.97 586.69 286.32 586.69 283.06 L 586.69 5.91 C 586.69 2.64 584.04 0 580.78 0 L 5.91 0 C 2.64 0 0 2.64 0 5.91 Z"></path></g><g style="--ltx-fill-color:#FFFFFF;" fill="#FFFFFF" fill-opacity="1.0"><path style="stroke:none" d="M 1.97 5.91 L 1.97 267.55 L 584.72 267.55 L 584.72 5.91 C 584.72 3.73 582.96 1.97 580.78 1.97 L 5.91 1.97 C 3.73 1.97 1.97 3.73 1.97 5.91 Z"></path></g><g fill-opacity="1.0" transform="matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 21.65 273.45)"><foreignObject style="--ltx-fo-width:34.15em;--ltx-fo-height:0.69em;--ltx-fo-depth:0em;font-size:10pt;" height="9.61" overflow="visible" transform="matrix(1 0 0 -1 0 9.61)" width="472.54"><span class="ltx_foreignobject_container"><span class="ltx_foreignobject_content">
<span id="A1.F22.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.1" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_minipage ltx_align_bottom" style="width:34.15em;">
<span id="A1.F22.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.F22.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold" style="--ltx-fg-color:#FFFFFF;">LLM Generated Research Idea</span></span>
</span></span></span></foreignObject></g><g fill-opacity="1.0" transform="matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 21.65 13.78)"><foreignObject style="--ltx-fo-width:39.27em;--ltx-fo-height:17.49em;--ltx-fo-depth:0em;font-size:10pt;" height="241.96" overflow="visible" transform="matrix(1 0 0 -1 0 241.96)" width="543.39"><span class="ltx_foreignobject_container"><span class="ltx_foreignobject_content">
<span id="A1.F22.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_minipage ltx_align_bottom" style="width:39.27em;">
<span id="A1.F22.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.F22.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">LLM:</span> Llama 3.1 405B-Instruct</span>
<span id="A1.F22.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.2" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.F22.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Resource Scenario:</span> High</span>
<span id="A1.F22.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.3" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.F22.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.3.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Generated Research idea:</span> The abstracts discuss various aspects of substance use disorder, including treatment approaches, harm reduction strategies, and the impact of the COVID-19 pandemic on overdose rates. They also touch on the importance of addressing social determinants and using robust research methods to inform public health policy. <span id="A1.F22.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.3.2" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-bg-color:#CCFFCC;">A novel hypothesis that emerges from these insights is that the implementation of a community-based, peer-led harm reduction program that incorporates prescription alternatives to illicit substances, along with social support and housing assistance, may lead to a significant reduction in overdose rates and improved health outcomes among individuals with substance use disorder.</span> This program would prioritize the needs of marginalized populations and address the root causes of addiction, such as poverty, trauma, and lack of access to healthcare. By leveraging the expertise of people with lived experience and fostering a sense of community and connection, this approach may offer a more effective and sustainable solution to the overdose crisis than traditional treatment models.</span>

</span></span></span></foreignObject></g><g fill-opacity="1.0" transform="matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 21.65 16.47)"><foreignObject style="--ltx-fo-width:39.27em;--ltx-fo-height:7.95em;--ltx-fo-depth:0.19em;font-size:10pt;" height="112.7" overflow="visible" transform="matrix(1 0 0 -1 0 110.01)" width="543.39"><span class="ltx_foreignobject_container"><span class="ltx_foreignobject_content">
<span id="A1.F23.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_minipage ltx_align_bottom" style="width:39.27em;">
<span id="A1.F23.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.F23.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2.1.1" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-bg-color:#FFCCCC;">Given that nucleus pulposus cells respond differently to temporary compression (TC) and sustained compression (SC) in the context of intervertebral disc degeneration, our research aims to elucidate the mechanisms governing autophagy and apoptosis under varying compression durations.</span> This study shows that SC leads to severe central NP cell degeneration and heightened apoptotic activity, whereas TC results in milder degeneration with a distinct peak in autophagy markers at 6 weeks. We propose to further explore TC as a model for early autophagy-mediated degeneration and SC as a model for late-stage, apoptosis-driven degeneration. This dual approach could help identify targeted interventions for different stages of disc degeneration.</span>

<span id="A1.F23.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_minipage ltx_align_bottom" style="width:39.27em;">
<span id="A1.F23.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.F23.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">LLM:</span> Gemini 1.5 Pro</span>
<span id="A1.F23.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.2" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.F23.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Resource Scenario:</span> High</span>
<span id="A1.F23.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.3" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.F23.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.3.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Generated Research idea:</span> Hypotheses: Given the established roles of autophagy and apoptosis in intervertebral disc degeneration (IVDD) and the emerging link between them under oxidative stress, we hypothesize that specific microRNAs (miRNAs) could regulate IVDD progression by modulating the crosstalk between these two pathways. Specifically, <span id="A1.F23.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.3.2" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-bg-color:#CCFFCC;">we propose that certain miRNAs, potentially upregulated in degenerative discs, could simultaneously suppress autophagy and promote apoptosis in nucleus pulposus cells, thereby exacerbating IVDD.</span> This hypothesis is based on the ability of miRNAs to target multiple genes within interconnected pathways and the evidence suggesting a protective role of autophagy in early IVDD stages, shifting towards a deleterious effect as apoptosis becomes dominant.</span>

<span id="A1.F23.p1.pic1.p3.pic1.2.1" class="ltx_p">1. Hypothesis D: Given the established roles of autophagy and apoptosis in intervertebral disc degeneration (IVDD) and the emerging link between them under oxidative stress, we hypothesize that specific microRNAs (miRNAs) could regulate IVDD progression by modulating the crosstalk between these two pathways. <span id="A1.F23.p1.pic1.p3.pic1.2.1.1" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-bg-color:#CCFFCC;">This hypothesis is highly novel due to its focus on miRNAs as regulators of the interplay between autophagy and apoptosis, which introduces a new layer of genetic regulation and potential therapeutic targets in IVDD.</span> …4. <span id="A1.F24.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.F24.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold" style="--ltx-fg-color:#FFFFFF;">Target Paper Research Idea</span></span>

<span id="A1.F24.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_minipage ltx_align_bottom" style="width:39.27em;">
<span id="A1.F24.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2.1" class="ltx_p">Given that insects utilize gustatory receptors (GRs) to detect various taste modalities, this study elucidates the structural mechanisms underlying sugar recognition and activation of two Drosophila sweet taste receptors, GR43a and GR64a. By using cryo-electron microscopy, the researchers revealed that both receptors form tetrameric sugar-gated cation channels, with sugar binding to ligand-binding domains inducing conformational changes that lead to channel opening. GR43a is specifically activated by the monosaccharide fructose, whereas GR64a responds to disaccharides like sucrose and maltose. <span id="A1.F24.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2.1.1" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-bg-color:#FFCCCC;">These findings provide structural insights into how different sugars are recognized by insect taste receptors, offering potential targets for designing insect attractants or repellents for pest control.</span></span>
</span></span></span></foreignObject></g></g></svg></span>
</span>
<span id="A1.F24.p1.pic1.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent"><span class="ltx_inline-block"><svg id="A1.F24.p1.pic1.p2.pic1" class="ltx_picture" color="#000000" height="175.43" overflow="visible" version="1.1" viewBox="0 0 586.69 175.43" width="586.69"><g style="--ltx-stroke-color:#000000;--ltx-fill-color:#000000;" fill="#000000" stroke="#000000" stroke-width="0.4pt" transform="translate(0,175.43) matrix(1 0 0 -1 0 0)"><g style="--ltx-fill-color:#000000;" fill="#000000" fill-opacity="1.0"><path style="stroke:none" d="M 0 5.91 L 0 169.52 C 0 172.78 2.64 175.43 5.91 175.43 L 580.78 175.43 C 584.04 175.43 586.69 172.78 586.69 169.52 L 586.69 5.91 C 586.69 2.64 584.04 0 580.78 0 L 5.91 0 C 2.64 0 0 2.64 0 5.91 Z"></path></g><g style="--ltx-fill-color:#FFFFFF;" fill="#FFFFFF" fill-opacity="1.0"><path style="stroke:none" d="M 1.97 5.91 L 1.97 154.01 L 584.72 154.01 L 584.72 5.91 C 584.72 3.73 582.96 1.97 580.78 1.97 L 5.91 1.97 C 3.73 1.97 1.97 3.73 1.97 5.91 Z"></path></g><g fill-opacity="1.0" transform="matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 21.65 159.91)"><foreignObject style="--ltx-fo-width:34.15em;--ltx-fo-height:0.69em;--ltx-fo-depth:0em;font-size:10pt;" height="9.61" overflow="visible" transform="matrix(1 0 0 -1 0 9.61)" width="472.54"><span class="ltx_foreignobject_container"><span class="ltx_foreignobject_content">
<span id="A1.F24.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.1" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_minipage ltx_align_bottom" style="width:34.15em;">
<span id="A1.F24.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.F24.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold" style="--ltx-fg-color:#FFFFFF;">LLM Generated Research Idea</span></span>
</span></span></span></foreignObject></g><g fill-opacity="1.0" transform="matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 21.65 16.47)"><foreignObject style="--ltx-fo-width:39.27em;--ltx-fo-height:9.09em;--ltx-fo-depth:0.19em;font-size:10pt;" height="128.42" overflow="visible" transform="matrix(1 0 0 -1 0 125.73)" width="543.39"><span class="ltx_foreignobject_container"><span class="ltx_foreignobject_content">
<span id="A1.F24.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_minipage ltx_align_bottom" style="width:39.27em;">
<span id="A1.F24.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.F24.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">LLM:</span> Gemini 1.5 Pro</span>
<span id="A1.F24.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.2" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.F24.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Resource Scenario:</span> High</span>
<span id="A1.F24.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.3" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.F24.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.3.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Generated Research idea:</span></span>
<span id="A1.F24.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.4" class="ltx_p">Given the prevalence of CH-<math id="A1.F24.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\pi" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>π</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\pi</annotation></semantics></math> interactions in carbohydrate recognition (Abstract 3) and the importance of taste perception in mosquito behavior (Abstract 4), <span id="A1.F24.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.4.1" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-bg-color:#CCFFCC;">we hypothesize that disrupting CH-<math id="A1.F24.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\pi" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi style="--ltx-bg-color:#CCFFCC;" mathbackground="#CCFFCC">π</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\pi</annotation></semantics></math> interactions between mosquito gustatory receptors and sugary food sources could lead to novel mosquito control strategies.</span></span>

<span id="A1.F24.p1.pic1.p3.pic1.2" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_minipage ltx_align_bottom" style="width:39.27em;">
<span id="A1.F24.p1.pic1.p3.pic1.2.1" class="ltx_p">1. Hypothesis B: Given the potential role of gustatory receptors like Gr43a in sensing internal nutrient levels, <span id="A1.F24.p1.pic1.p3.pic1.2.1.1" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-bg-color:#CCFFCC;">this hypothesis explores a novel and unexpected connection between taste receptors and pancreatic beta cell function, suggesting a role in type 2 diabetes. This represents a unique cross-disciplinary insight with significant implications for understanding diabetes.</span> … 4. Hypothesis A: While the study of the structural mechanisms underlying sugar recognition and activation of Drosophila sweet taste receptors is valuable, <span id="A1.F24.p1.pic1.p3.pic1.2.1.2" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-bg-color:#FFCCCC;">it is more of an extension of existing knowledge rather than a groundbreaking new idea.</span> The use of cryo-electron microscopy to reveal these mechanisms is an important but expected application of this technology in structural biology.</span>

<span id="A1.F25.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.F25.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold" style="--ltx-fg-color:#FFFFFF;">Target Paper Research Idea</span></span>
</span></span></span></foreignObject></g><g fill-opacity="1.0" transform="matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 21.65 16.47)"><foreignObject style="--ltx-fo-width:39.27em;--ltx-fo-height:9.09em;--ltx-fo-depth:0.19em;font-size:10pt;" height="128.53" overflow="visible" transform="matrix(1 0 0 -1 0 125.84)" width="543.39"><span class="ltx_foreignobject_container"><span class="ltx_foreignobject_content">
<span id="A1.F25.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_minipage ltx_align_bottom" style="width:39.27em;">
<span id="A1.F25.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2.1" class="ltx_p">Given that effectively mitigating climate change necessitates significant global behavior change, our research aims to identify the most effective strategies to motivate shifts in climate beliefs and actions. A recent study tested 11 different interventions across a large international sample and found that the interventions varied in effectiveness depending on the targeted outcome and the participants’ initial climate beliefs. <span id="A1.F25.p1.pic1.p1.pic1.2.1.1" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-bg-color:#FFCCCC;">Specifically, interventions were moderately successful in enhancing climate beliefs and policy support among non-skeptics, but had limited impact on more effortful behaviors like tree planting.</span> These findings highlight the need for tailored interventions that consider the audience’s predispositions and the specific behavioral goals.</span>
</span></span></span></foreignObject></g></g></svg></span>
</span>

<span id="A1.F25.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.F25.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">LLM:</span> Gemini 1.5 Pro</span>
<span id="A1.F25.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.2" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.F25.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Resource Scenario:</span> High</span>
<span id="A1.F25.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.3" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.F25.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.3.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Generated Research idea:</span></span>
<span id="A1.F25.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.4" class="ltx_p">Hypothesis: <span id="A1.F25.p1.pic1.p2.pic1.2.4.1" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-bg-color:#CCFFCC;">Framing climate change mitigation behaviors as collective actions that reduce psychological distance (e.g., emphasizing local community efforts with immediate and tangible benefits) will increase individual participation in pro-environmental behaviors, particularly among individuals who score high on measures of climate change helplessness.</span></span>

<span id="A1.F25.p1.pic1.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent"><span class="ltx_inline-block"><svg id="A1.F25.p1.pic1.p3.pic1" class="ltx_picture" color="#000000" height="161.63" overflow="visible" version="1.1" viewBox="0 0 586.69 161.63" width="586.69"><g style="--ltx-stroke-color:#000000;--ltx-fill-color:#000000;" fill="#000000" stroke="#000000" stroke-width="0.4pt" transform="translate(0,161.63) matrix(1 0 0 -1 0 0)"><g style="--ltx-fill-color:#000000;" fill="#000000" fill-opacity="1.0"><path style="stroke:none" d="M 0 5.91 L 0 155.72 C 0 158.98 2.64 161.63 5.91 161.63 L 580.78 161.63 C 584.04 161.63 586.69 158.98 586.69 155.72 L 586.69 5.91 C 586.69 2.64 584.04 0 580.78 0 L 5.91 0 C 2.64 0 0 2.64 0 5.91 Z"></path></g><g style="--ltx-fill-color:#FFFFFF;" fill="#FFFFFF" fill-opacity="1.0"><path style="stroke:none" d="M 1.97 5.91 L 1.97 137.52 L 584.72 137.52 L 584.72 5.91 C 584.72 3.73 582.96 1.97 580.78 1.97 L 5.91 1.97 C 3.73 1.97 1.97 3.73 1.97 5.91 Z"></path></g><g fill-opacity="1.0" transform="matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 21.65 146.11)"><foreignObject style="--ltx-fo-width:34.15em;--ltx-fo-height:0.69em;--ltx-fo-depth:0.19em;font-size:10pt;" height="12.3" overflow="visible" transform="matrix(1 0 0 -1 0 9.61)" width="472.54"><span class="ltx_foreignobject_container"><span class="ltx_foreignobject_content">

<span id="A1.F25.p1.pic1.p3.pic1.2.1" class="ltx_p">1. Hypothesis D: Framing climate change mitigation behaviors as collective actions that reduce psychological distance is a novel approach, particularly targeting individuals who feel helpless about climate change. <span id="A1.F25.p1.pic1.p3.pic1.2.1.1" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-bg-color:#CCFFCC;">This combines psychological concepts with community-focused interventions, offering a fresh perspective on motivating pro-environmental behaviors.</span> … 4. Hypothesis A: Identifying effective strategies to motivate shifts in climate beliefs and actions based on audience predispositions and specific behavioral goals <span id="A1.F25.p1.pic1.p3.pic1.2.1.2" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-bg-color:#FFCCCC;">is important but more incremental. The rationale and findings suggest extensions of existing research rather than a fundamentally new approach.</span></span>
